# Valuation

Inputs and output for the Valuation pass. EV/sales and P/E are recomputed at prices as of the November 25, 2026 close (FMP quote; refreshed from the November 20 close for every row), using the screener's sales, earnings and net debt; the 1-year price change and distance from the 52-week high are from FMP as of the same close. Nubank (NU) is not in the screener: its EV/sales and P/E use its FMP statements, and as a bank its EV/sales (debt and cash include deposits and lending) means little. Gross margin vs 5-yr low is from the screener (mid-November 2026). News Sentiment is from `data/screener/AI Subsectors/News/News Summary.md`; Potential growth from `data/screener/Potential Growth/`. A dash means not available (P/E is blank when net income is zero or negative). RELX, LSEGY and SBGSY report half-yearly: their figures use their last two half-years as the trailing year and their latest half-year in place of a quarter (corrected 11-23-26).

**Legend:** each Valuation ends with one of three judgments. *Little* of the expected growth is in the price: the multiples look low for the growth. *Part*: the multiples are reasonable for the growth. *Much*: the multiples already pay for the growth continuing, leaving little room for a shortfall.

| # | Symbol | Company | EV/sales | P/E | 1-yr price change | vs 52-week high | Gross margin vs 5-yr low | News Sentiment | Potential growth | Valuation |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | AAON | AAON, Inc. | 3.0x | 31.6 | -19.0% | -49.7% | -0.3pp | Coverage after Q3 (Nov 9) is strongly positive: reports said shares jumped about 22% premarket and 37.8% for the week, Zacks upgraded it to Strong Buy, Seeking Alpha upgraded it, and the analyst consensus was reported as Buy. The same coverage noted AAON restated Q1 and Q2 results after finding accounting errors and identified a material weakness in internal controls, and a September piece cited negative free cash flow and margin pressure from capacity expansion. Reports also said the stock had fallen about 25% year to date before the report and that insiders made 15 sales and two purchases over six months. Coverage is moderate at 27 items. | Cooling demand grows as data centers are finished and accelerators get denser: liquid cooling grows with density, and air cooling grows with every new facility. AAON reaches this through BASX, its data center line, which rose 218% to $397.2M in Q3 2026 and is now more than half of revenue, so most of the company's growth now depends on data center cooling. The inputs do not split BASX between liquid and air; from background knowledge rather than the inputs, BASX makes both coolant distribution units and air handlers, which would place it in both parts of the Upside.<br><br>Against trailing sales of $2.3B, the opportunity is large. Q3 revenue of $707.4M, multiplied by four, is about $2.8B a year, and the $1.9B backlog, up 40.5%, equals most of a year of trailing sales. With repeat hyperscaler orders and new customer wins cited, the inference is that orders are still building.<br><br>Current growth shows the company already capturing much of this. Sales rose 71.5% over the trailing year and 84.1% in the latest quarter, up from about 9% a year earlier, and gross margin is below its five-year range, so the growth looks like volume rather than price and is not inflated by unusual margins. A large part of the potential growth is already visible. More appears still ahead, since the trailing figure lags the latest quarter and the backlog covers much of the coming year, though on a larger base the pace would likely be below the current step-up. | The market pays 2.9x EV/sales and 31.4 times earnings. Against the growth, the sales multiple is modest: sales rose 71.5% over the trailing year and 84.1% in the latest quarter, and Q3 revenue annualizes to about $2.8B, so on the current run rate EV/sales is closer to 2.4x, with a $1.9B backlog covering most of a year of trailing sales. The P/E is higher than the sales multiple alone suggests, but it is measured with gross margin 0.3pp below its five-year low, so today's margins do not flatter the multiples; if capacity expansion costs ease, earnings could rise faster than sales and the P/E would look lower on normal margins. That is an inference, and the restated Q1 and Q2 results and the reported material weakness in controls make the earnings figure less reliable than usual, which is a reason to lean on EV/sales here.<br><br>The price path points the same way. The stock is 19.6% lower over the year and still 50.0% below its 52-week high, even after the reported 37.8% weekly jump following Q3, while the growth read says the business has accelerated rather than weakened. This looks like a fall that came with stronger growth, not weaker, and the strongly positive post-Q3 sentiment has so far recovered only part of it. The caveats are real: negative free cash flow, the accounting issues, and a pace that will likely slow on a larger base.<br><br>Overall, little of the expected growth appears to be in the price. A modest sales multiple, margins at a low rather than a peak, and a price still half its high together suggest the market is not paying for the data center growth already visible in the run rate and backlog, though the control weakness and slowing pace on a larger base are reasons it may be discounting it. |
| 2 | AMAT | Applied Materials, Inc. | 7.1x | 24.5 | +23.4% | -57.9% | +3.5pp | Coverage is mixed: commentary on the business is positive while the stock has been weak. Reports said shares were down about 48% from their June peak by late October amid a semiconductor sell-off, and fell about 6% after the record Q4 as investors focused on supply constraints (cleanroom space and components), a lower China share and concern that budgets tilt toward lithography; at least 10 firms cut price targets citing valuation while keeping Buy-equivalent ratings. Motley Fool and Zacks pieces call it a top AI chip stock and 'too cheap to ignore,' while one report said its equipment market share fell from 20% in 2024 to 17.9% in 2025. | Wafer and packaging demand tracks accelerator volume with supply tight, so equipment suppliers gain from more wafers and more advanced packaging; in memory, makers are adding capacity in every tier, and equipment suppliers gain from that volume. Applied Materials supplies equipment to both, and chipmaking equipment and services are its whole business, so its growth depends directly on how much its customers build. The AI Role places it where the Upside says demand is growing: management expects semiconductor systems to grow more than 40% in 2026 and advanced-packaging revenue to grow above 70%, and its research partners include the leading foundry and the main memory makers. On sales of $34.4B, it takes a large opportunity to move growth, and this one spans logic, memory and packaging together.<br><br>Current growth shows the upside arriving only recently. Sales grew 21.4% over the trailing year but 53.1% in the latest quarter, up from a slight decline a year earlier, and part of that jump may reflect comparison with a weak quarter. Gross margin is only modestly above its low, so the growth does not look price-driven. FQ4 revenue of $10.41B, multiplied by four, is about $41.6B a year, roughly a fifth above trailing sales, so much of the near-term gain is already set by the current run rate but not yet in the trailing figure. A good part of the potential growth is visible in the latest quarter and still ahead in the trailing numbers; growth beyond that depends on customers continuing to add capacity at the pace management describes. | The market pays 6.9x EV/sales and 23.8 times earnings. Set against the growth, these look moderate rather than stretched: sales grew 21.4% over the trailing year but 53.1% in the latest quarter, and FQ4 revenue annualizes to about $41.6B, roughly a fifth above trailing sales, so on the run rate EV/sales is nearer 5.7x and the P/E would fall further if earnings follow. Management expects semiconductor systems to grow more than 40% in 2026 and advanced packaging above 70%. Gross margin is 3.5pp above its five-year low, so current margins flatter the multiples only slightly; at the low, the P/E would be somewhat higher but not dramatically so.<br><br>The price path is the striking part. The stock is up 31.1% over the year but 59.1% below its 52-week high, which means it rose to roughly three times its year-ago price and then gave most of that back; news reports a fall of about 48% from the June peak by late October and a further drop after a record quarter. Sentiment is mixed, with at least 10 firms cutting targets on valuation while keeping Buy-equivalent ratings, and concerns centered on supply constraints, China and lithography's share of budgets. The growth read, by contrast, shows growth only recently arriving and still building, so this looks closer to a fall in mood than a fall in the business, although the reported market-share loss and the cyclical nature of equipment spending are reasons the market may be cautious.<br><br>Overall, part of the expected growth appears to be in the price. The multiples on the current run rate leave room for the growth management describes, provided customers keep adding capacity at that pace. |
| 3 | AMZN | Amazon.com, Inc. | 3.5x | 19.6 | +4.8% | -14.9% | +9.3pp | Coverage is mixed. Bullish pieces point to AWS acceleration and the backlog (Evercore raised its target to $355, citing agentic AI), while others question the $220B spending, record cash burn and return on investment. Reports said shares slid in the November AI and mega-cap sell-off, Third Point exited its Amazon stake, and one article said Amazon had underperformed other hyperscalers. Most of the 54 Perigon stories in the file are unrelated retail and consumer items. | Amazon's main AI position is AWS as a cloud provider in runtime, where demand grows as agents are adopted and as more models and accelerators are in use. AWS demand is running ahead of supply: revenue grew 37% to $42.2B in Q2, the backlog is $496B to $578B, and capacity is constrained into 2027, with about $220B of capex this year and two million more Nvidia GPUs planned for 2027-2028. Its Trainium chips sit in the mixed-fleet growth in processors, and the inference is that this shows up mainly through AWS rather than as separate sales. Its positions in data (marketplace data and AWS databases), tools (the marketplace as a destination for shopping agents) and trust (security services and Guardrails, from background knowledge) follow the same agent-driven demand but are small or unsized in the inputs.<br><br>Size limits how much this moves the whole company. Q2 AWS revenue of $42.2B, multiplied by four, is about $169B a year, roughly a fifth of $797.5B in trailing sales, so even fast AWS growth adds only about a fifth of its rate to total growth, and the much larger retail business sets most of the rest. This explains the gap between AWS's 37% and total growth of 15.3% over the trailing year and 12.1% in the latest quarter; the AWS figure better reflects the AI business. Gross margin is meaningfully above its low, which may mean part of the total growth comes from mix or price rather than volume.<br><br>The AI upside is visible inside AWS but only partly in total growth. Much of it appears still ahead, since the backlog is about three times AWS's annual run rate and growth is held back by capacity the planned capex is meant to add, but its effect on total sales is diluted by the size of the rest of the business. | The market pays 3.5x EV/sales and 19.8 times earnings. For a company growing 15.3% over the trailing year and 12.1% in the latest quarter, those multiples are not demanding, but the total growth understates the AI business: AWS grew 37% in Q2 and is only about a fifth of sales, with a backlog of $496B to $578B, roughly three times its annual run rate. The multiples therefore mostly price the larger retail business, and the AWS growth still ahead is only partly reflected in either figure.<br><br>Margins need care. Gross margin is 9.3pp above its five-year low, so the multiples look cheaper than they would at that low. From background knowledge rather than the inputs, much of that rise reflects a mix shift toward AWS, advertising and services, which would make it more durable than a cyclical peak, but if margins fell back the P/E would be noticeably higher. A second point the P/E misses is cash: about $220B of capex this year, with record cash burn reported, means earnings overstate what shareholders currently receive, and rising depreciation from that spending will weigh on future earnings. The moderate P/E may be the market's way of discounting that spending until returns are visible.<br><br>The price path is modest: up 9.1% over the year and 14.1% below its 52-week high, with mixed sentiment that weighs AWS acceleration against spending and return questions. Neither the rise nor the fall is large, so the price does not appear to have moved far ahead of or behind the business.<br><br>Overall, part of the expected growth appears to be in the price. The multiples do not look stretched for the AWS backlog and acceleration, but margins above their low and heavy capex mean part of the apparent cheapness depends on that spending earning its return. |
| 4 | ANET | Arista Networks, Inc. | 16.2x | 43.6 | +25.5% | -24.6% | +1.8pp | Analyst coverage is broadly positive, with recurring valuation and insider-selling caveats. After Q3, JPMorgan raised its target to $270 and Needham reiterated Buy at $260, and Bernstein initiated at Outperform in September; Wells Fargo trimmed its target to $245, and Zacks rated it #1 (Strong Buy). Reports said the stock hit highs near $215-217 in early October, then fell nearly 20% from those highs in the AI sell-off (Seeking Alpha, Oct 19), swung after the Q3 report and dropped 3% with Cisco on Nov 13. Caveats in the coverage include a trailing P/E of about 64 against a five-year median of 42, a GuruFocus DCF value of $90, gross margin falling to 64%, slower sequential growth, customer concentration, no Ethernet scale-up revenue expected until trials in 2027, heavy pre-planned insider selling (GuruFocus cited $1.55B over 12 months, including sales by CEO Jayshree Ullal and Andreas Bechtolsheim), and two actively exploited VeloCloud vulnerabilities in September and October. Coverage is heavy at 77 items, though many Perigon stories are institutional-holding and insider filings. | Networking demand grows as more accelerators are deployed and clusters get larger: scale out adds links, scale across grows as computing spreads across sites, and inference adds demand because faster links work around memory limits. Arista sits at all three levels. Its AI fabrics are scale out, the Etherlink SU-144 is an entry into scale up, and an executive says about 30% of its AI business is already scale across. Networking is its whole business, and AI is a growing share, with at least $3.6B of AI revenue expected in 2026, about a quarter of the $13.3B outlook, and AI-fabrics customers grown from a handful to more than 100.<br><br>Against trailing sales of $11.9B, the company's 2027 target of about $18.5B is about 39% above the 2026 outlook, and its $7B from AI fabrics would be more than a third of revenue. These are company targets, not independent estimates.<br><br>Current growth shows the company already capturing much of this. Sales grew 40.7% over the trailing year and 58.5% in the latest quarter, both accelerating, and gross margin near its low gives no sign of price-driven growth. Q3 revenue of $3.66B, multiplied by four, is about $14.6B a year, already above the 2026 outlook, so part of the coming year's growth is set by the current run rate. A substantial part of the potential growth is visible now; the 2027 targets and the newer scale-up and scale-across lines point to further growth still ahead, with scale up barely in current sales. | The market pays 16.6x EV/sales and 44.6 times earnings, high multiples by any measure. The growth behind them is strong: sales rose 40.7% over the trailing year and 58.5% in the latest quarter, and Q3 revenue annualizes to about $14.6B, so on the run rate EV/sales is about 13.5x. On the company's 2027 target of about $18.5B it would be closer to 11x, but that target is the company's own and assumes the newer scale-up and scale-across lines deliver. Even granting that, the multiples ask for the growth to continue at a high rate for several years.<br><br>Margins do not flatter the figures much. Gross margin is only 1.8pp above its five-year low, and news reports it falling to 64%, so the earnings behind the P/E are not inflated by peak margins; if anything, further mix toward AI and large customers could hold margins down rather than lift them.<br><br>The price path suggests much of this is already recognized. The stock is up 37.2% over the year, a large rise accompanied by broadly positive sentiment and Strong Buy ratings, though it is 22.9% below its 52-week high after the October AI sell-off. The fall from the high came with growth still accelerating, which takes some heat out of the price, but coverage repeatedly cites valuation, customer concentration, heavy insider selling and no Ethernet scale-up revenue until trials in 2027.<br><br>Overall, much of the expected growth appears to be in the price. The exceptional, accelerating growth goes some way to justifying the multiples, and the pullback from the high has trimmed them, but at about 13x run-rate sales the price leaves little room for a shortfall against the 2027 targets. |
| 5 | APH | Amphenol Corporation | 5.8x | 30.4 | +3.7% | -18.7% | +7.5pp | Coverage is mostly positive. Reports said shares rose about 6% after Q3 and 12.7% over two days, Zacks upgraded it to Rank #1 (Strong Buy) in November, and the analyst consensus was reported as Moderate Buy to Strong Buy, with JPMorgan, UBS, Barclays and BNP Paribas Exane raising targets and Wolfe, Bernstein and Erste initiating at Buy-equivalent ratings; Rothschild Redburn and TD Cowen cut targets, TD Cowen to Hold. Bull arguments cite denser AI systems adding connection content, record orders, the CommScope upside, a $4B buyback and a 20% dividend increase. Caveats include valuation (GuruFocus DCF values of $50-57 against prices near $77-88), EPS of $0.80 against $0.93 a year earlier, $17.9B of debt, dependence on AI spending (one Seeking Alpha author withheld a Strong Buy for that reason), heavy put buying in October, and steady insider selling, including CEO Richard Norwitt's $65.9M sale on Nov 2. Coverage is heavy at 67 items, though many are institutional-holding filings and Zacks screen pieces. | Amphenol sits in two parts of the Upside. In networking, demand grows as accelerators are linked within and between racks; its connectors and cable assemblies serve copper at short reach, and the CommScope acquisition and high-density optics work add optical links beyond the rack. In electrical equipment, the busbars and power distribution systems in its description serve distribution inside the site, where demand rises with power per rack, but the inputs give no figures, so this looks like a minor part. IT datacom is about 43% of sales and AI about three-quarters of that, so multiplying the two puts AI at roughly a third of sales; IT datacom grew 77% organically in Q3. The rest is diversified, including defense growing 30%.<br><br>On trailing sales of $33.0B, a third is roughly $11B a year of AI business, so its growth moves the total substantially, and record orders of $12.2B against Q3 revenue of $10.23B suggest demand still running ahead of shipments.<br><br>Current growth shows the upside already being captured, but reported figures overstate it. Sales grew 57.6% over the trailing year and 65.1% in the latest quarter, while Q3 organic growth was 37%; the difference is acquisitions, and organic growth better reflects what continues. Gross margin is noticeably above its low, so some growth may come from higher prices. Much of the potential growth is already visible. More appears ahead in AI datacom as orders exceed revenue, while the acquisition boost to reported growth would fade once those deals have been in the base for a full year. | The market pays 5.6x EV/sales and 29.0 times earnings. Reported growth is very high, 57.6% over the trailing year and 65.1% in the latest quarter, and Q3 revenue of $10.23B annualizes to about $40.9B, so EV/sales on the run rate is nearer 4.5x. But the reported figures include acquisitions, which also add to EV through $17.9B of debt; organic growth was 37% in Q3, and that is the rate more likely to continue. Against organic growth in the high 30s driven by AI datacom, and orders of $12.2B running ahead of revenue, the multiples look reasonable rather than stretched.<br><br>Margins flatter them somewhat. Gross margin is 7.5pp above its five-year low, which suggests some of the growth is price or mix; if margins fell back toward the low, the P/E would be meaningfully higher than 29. News also reports EPS of $0.80 against $0.93 a year earlier, which the inputs do not explain; the inference is that acquisition-related costs weighed on reported earnings, which would make the P/E look higher than underlying earnings justify.<br><br>The price path is notable for how little it has moved: up 1.1% over the year while reported sales grew more than half, which implies the multiples have compressed substantially. It is also 22.5% below its 52-week high. Sentiment is mostly positive, so the flat price does not look like a reaction to weakening growth; caveats in coverage center on valuation, debt and dependence on AI spending.<br><br>Overall, part of the expected growth appears to be in the price. A flat price against strong organic growth and rising orders suggests the market has not followed the business up, though margins above their low and acquisition-boosted figures mean the multiples are less cheap than they first appear. |
| 6 | AVGO | Broadcom Inc. | 16.5x | 37.5 | -25.3% | -39.2% | +7.4pp | Coverage is sharply mixed: analysts stay bullish on the business while the stock has been weak. Reports said none of 51 analysts rate it Sell (Fox Advisors has a Street-high $715 target, Goldman a $460 target, and DA Davidson upgraded it to Buy), and several Seeking Alpha and Motley Fool pieces call it cheap at about 17-19x forward earnings. The stock was reported about 30% below its June record in September, down 5.8% on Nov 18 after a Rothschild Redburn downgrade and target cuts, and down about 11-12% on Nov 20 (losing $191B, 36% below its record) after analyst Ming-Chi Kuo reported that Google cut about 1 million Broadcom chips from its 2027 plans in favor of MediaTek. Caveats in the coverage include customer concentration (one customer was reported at 50% of revenue), vendor-financed demand and off-balance-sheet exposure (estimates of up to about $72B in Anthropic backstops, CDS widening in August), memory-heavy XPUs pressuring gross margin, exploited VMware vCenter vulnerabilities, China reviewing Broadcom switches in state data centers, and Stanley Druckenmiller's exit. Coverage is heavy at 107 items, though many Perigon stories are market-wide or about other companies. | In processors, accelerator demand grows with the number bought, and mixed fleets spread that growth to custom ASICs alongside GPUs; in networking, demand grows as clusters get larger and every added accelerator has to be linked in. Broadcom sits in both as a designer of custom accelerators (XPUs) and AI networking chips for six major buyers, and AI semiconductors are already the larger part of the business: $16.7B in fiscal Q3 2026, up 221%, more than half of total revenue of $29.6B. The rest is non-AI semiconductors and VMware software, which the inputs do not size separately.<br><br>Against trailing sales of $89.1B, the company's own outlook is very large: AI revenue of $58B in FY2026, about $115B in FY2027 and $230B in FY2028, roughly doubling each year, with the CEO saying supply is secured. These are company targets, not independent estimates. Named programs with OpenAI and Anthropic, and remaining performance obligations of $179.2B, about twice trailing sales, support the inference that the demand is contracted rather than hoped for.<br><br>Current growth shows the upside arriving fast but not yet in the trailing figure. Sales grew 48.7% over the trailing year and 85.5% in the latest quarter, up from about 22% a year earlier. Q3 revenue of $29.6B, multiplied by four, is about $118B a year, about a third above trailing sales, and Q4 AI guidance of $21.7B is higher again. Gross margin is noticeably above its low, so part of the growth may come from price or mix. A meaningful part of the potential growth is visible in the latest quarters, but if the company's outlook holds, most of it is still ahead. | The market pays 16.7x EV/sales and 38.0 times earnings, high multiples on trailing figures. The growth is moving fast, though: sales grew 48.7% over the trailing year and 85.5% in the latest quarter, and Q3 revenue of $29.6B annualizes to about $118B, which would put EV/sales at about 12.6x on the current run rate. The company's outlook of AI revenue roughly doubling each year, to about $115B in FY2027, would lower it much further if it holds, and remaining performance obligations of $179.2B, about twice trailing sales, give that outlook some backing. The P/E is based on reported (GAAP) earnings, and from background knowledge Broadcom's reported earnings carry heavy amortization from the VMware acquisition, so the P/E likely looks higher than underlying earnings would suggest. VMware has been in the base for well over a year, so it no longer distorts EV/sales.<br><br>Gross margin is 7.4pp above its five-year low, so the multiples look somewhat cheaper than they would at normal margins, and news cites memory-heavy XPUs pressuring gross margin, which points to margins moving down rather than up as the AI share rises.<br><br>The price is 19.5% lower over the year and 38.5% below its 52-week high. Part of that looks like mood, with analysts uniformly bullish on the business, but the Nov 20 drop came on a report that Google cut about 1 million chips from its 2027 plans. That is a direct threat to the outlook rather than sentiment alone, and with one customer reported at about half of revenue, concentration is the main way a shortfall would arrive.<br><br>Overall, part of the expected growth appears to be in the price if the company's outlook holds. On the run rate and the outlook the multiples look moderate, but they rest heavily on a few customers delivering on plans that are already being questioned. |
| 7 | CEG | Constellation Energy Corporation | 3.1x | 19.1 | -35.4% | -38.2% | +22.2pp | Coverage turned more positive from October after a weak stretch. Reports said shares were down about 23-30% year to date by late September on valuation and on doubts about how fast AI demand reaches earnings (Q2 net income fell to $513M from $839M), then jumped about 12% on the Google deal and rose after the Q3 beat; HSBC initiated at Buy with a $335 target, Morgan Stanley raised its target, and Seeking Alpha pieces reaffirmed Buy. GuruFocus reported insider buying and no insider sales over 12 months. Caveats include regulatory and PJM grid-connection delays, Crane restart timing, Calpine-related debt and integration, negative trailing free cash flow (about -$49M), outages, a premium P/E of about 27, and Steve Eisman's warning that local governments slowing data-center construction is the biggest AI risk. Coverage is heavy at 72 items, though several Perigon stories are unrelated (Constellation Brands, Constellation Software, a Silent Hill game guide). | Power demand grows as data centers are planned and built, and some is contracted before sites are finished. The Upside puts near-term on-site demand with natural gas and treats nuclear as further out but still of near-term value. Constellation's AI position is as the largest US nuclear operator selling carbon-free power to hyperscalers, and most of what the inputs describe is existing output placed under long contracts: 2,700 MW from the existing fleet for Google, alongside added capacity from uprates (890 MW for Google, 190 MW at Calvert Cliffs for Amazon, about 1.1 GW under a DOE loan guarantee) and the Crane restart targeted for 2027.<br><br>Against a 32,400 MW portfolio and $34.2B of trailing sales, the added capacity is modest. Summing the uprates named in the inputs gives roughly 2.2 GW, about 7% of the portfolio, plus Crane, which from background knowledge is about 0.8 GW. The inference is that AI adds to growth mainly through higher contracted prices on existing output and, more slowly, through uprates and the restart as they come online.<br><br>Current growth overstates what AI has delivered so far. Sales grew 37.9% over the trailing year and 45.4% in the latest quarter, up from roughly flat, but Q3 was helped by the Calpine acquisition, which from background knowledge is mainly a gas-fired generator, and gross margin is 22.2 points above its five-year low, so much of the growth may come from higher prices that could prove temporary. Little of the AI-driven volume growth is visible yet; most of it, from uprates and the restart, is still ahead, while the acquisition boost to reported growth would fade after a full year in the base. | The market pays 3.1x EV/sales and 19.3 times earnings. These look modest, but two features of the inputs mean they need adjusting. First, gross margin is 22.2pp above its five-year low, a very large gap, so today's earnings reflect power prices well above where they have been; if margins moved back toward the low, the P/E would be much higher than 19.3. The lower P/E may partly mean the market is already discounting that. Second, the P/E uses reported (GAAP) net income, which from background knowledge includes swings from hedges and nuclear decommissioning fund returns, and news cites Q2 net income falling to $513M from $839M, so it is a noisy measure here.<br><br>The Calpine acquisition distorts EV/sales. EV includes the acquired debt in full, while trailing sales include Calpine only since it closed, so the multiple likely looks higher than it will once a full year of Calpine sales is in the base. At the same time, reported growth of 37.9% over the trailing year and 45.4% in the latest quarter is inflated by that deal, and the growth read says little AI-driven volume is visible yet; uprates of roughly 2.2 GW and the Crane restart in 2027 are still ahead. The inference is that the long hyperscaler contracts, such as the 2,700 MW for Google, lock in part of today's higher pricing, which would make the elevated margins more durable than a spot-price spike.<br><br>The price is 33.0% lower over the year and 37.7% below its 52-week high, after a fall that news ties to valuation and doubts about how fast AI reaches earnings, followed by more positive coverage from October. The growth behind the price has not weakened, so the fall looks more like mood than business.<br><br>Overall, part of the expected growth appears to be in the price. The fall has taken out much of the premium, but the multiples depend on margins far above their low persisting, which contracted pricing supports only in part. |

The company's 2029 target of $1.1-1.3B, about 1.5 to 1.8 times trailing sales, would put EV/sales near 2x on that figure if it held. About 2.3 times run-rate sales for a recovery that has not yet turned into earnings pays for little acceleration.<br><br>The price is 24.0% higher over the year and 58.2% below its 52-week high. Coverage is sparse and neutral, with a Hold consensus and GuruFocus calling it overvalued in October. The pending merger with Axcelis, expected to close by the end of Q1 2027, shapes the price: from background knowledge, it is an all-stock deal, so Veeco's price largely moves with Axcelis's and the combined company's prospects. Axcelis is also of similar or larger size, so reported growth after closing will include a large lift that is not organic.<br><br>Overall, part of the expected growth appears to be in the price, but the price is a weak signal of the market's view of Veeco itself, because the merger ties it to Axcelis. On its own figures, about 2.3 times run-rate sales leaves the 2027 acceleration largely unpriced, while thin earnings and weak margins explain why the market is not paying more. |
